Simpler spelling 

All Script Shorthand publications use the 12 words adopted by the National Education Association in 1898, and used in all N E A publications, which include tho tives. thru thoro program catalog and their deriva- A shorthand systen, which writes consistently by sound, should assuredly uphold to this extent, at least, the tremendously important movement for the reform of English spelling which commands the united support of loading language scholars both of England and America. 

Simplified Spelling Board, Lake Placid Club, NY, will send full information concerning this important movement on request.

Script Shorthand Dictionary Preface 

This dictionary is intended primarily for the teacher and for occasional reference, rather than for the student or writer or for constant reference. 

In several respects, a dictionary of Script Shorthand forms is of relatively limited importance; for Script 

Shorthand does not have many reporting breves which must be learned more or less individually, nor many rules to be applied, sometimes with the necessity for choosing between them, nor many forms which must be somewhat arbitrarily distinguished to avoid conflict, nor many signs or forms of more than one meaning. The chief opportunity for uncertainty in writing Script Shorthand forms is whether to write or reduce or omit an unaccented medial vowel; and Script Shorthand forms are, in general, so full and explicit that, if the writer writes any given word always the same (thus avoiding mental hesitation), noither he nor any other writer is likely to find the slightest difficulty in reading it, whichever decision has been made. For teaching purposes, however, there le some justification for a conveniently accessible authority, to give consistency and uniformity. pose this dictionery seeks to serve, That pur The Introduction calls attention to some of the genoral principles which have guided the choice of forms, together with some of the minor changes made in the interosts of consistency and uniformity. It also presents in convenient form for reference or drill an additional group of suggested short forms, written in accord with general text-book principles; and calls attention to the few irregular forms which should receive specific attention. 

The author will welcome comments or suggostions

Script Shorthand Dictionary regarding the present tentative edition, which may help to determine whether a definitive edition is justified or what it should contain. For convenience in noting additional words which may have been looked for but not found, blank lines at the bottom of each page have been provided. 

Lake Placid Club, NY 

February, 1941

Godfrey Dewey

Several thousand additional words selected from sources likely to yield material of value to the shorthand teacher. 

Spelling and pronunciation

The authority followed in such matters as spelling, division of syllables, compounding of words, and pronunciation is, in most cases, the Funk & Wagnalls New Standard Dictionary. In a very few cases this Script Shorthand Dictionary has preferred the second choice of the Standard and/or the first choice of hebster. 

Vowel markings

In the shorthand forms given in this dictionary, the dot which distinguishes long vowels from short vowels is written in most cases where there is a familiar word with the corresponding short vowel. In ordinary connected matter many of these dots will usually be unnecessary. The small circle which distinguishes definitely a short vowel is written much less frequently, even in the Dictionary; in general, only when the short vowel word is clearly of infrequent occurrence. 

Hyphened words The shorthand forms for hyphened words are normally written joined together like a phrase. Where the resulting form does not seem unmistakably legible, the hyphen may be writton directly above the joining. (This is clearly set forth in Dictation 

Course R17f, but not specifically mentioned in Text or Teacher.)

Where the compound form would extend too far above or below the line of writing, the words are written separately, with the hyphen between them, just as in longhand.

vi 

 Lineality 

In the very few cases where the form for a single word would extend more than 3 units above the line of writing, or more than 3 units below the line, the form is written in two parts, close together, side by side. (Text 78a and Dictation Course R14c specify 3 units above as well as below the line. Only a few words heretofore taught are affected by this change.) 

Position of prefixes 

The basic rule of position, for all forms, is: Write the first down or up stroke of a word resting on the line of writing, actual or assumed---if not more than 2 signs which are not down or up strokes precede it. тв a) ве b) uns poken reconstruction unserviceable ve re approbation conservation reexport reproduce excommunicate a)

In applying this basic rule, the prefix un- is disre garded (Text 4lal, Dictation Course R14al). 

b) Disregard also the 7 prefixes---in-, en, con-, com-, OK-, pro- tele- ---which may be raised (or lowered) to conform to the position of a following sign. c) reliable renow reappear :recite :reassume :relax لها surpass :surcease c) Prefixes other than un." which do not involve a downstroke or upstroke (note especially re-) follow the basic rule of position. مری میاد عي ތ ވގ a) e) incompetent self-control self-reliance inconspicuous self-defense inexpensive self-interest a) Where in- is followed by another of the liftable prefixes, begin the in- stroke on the line (Compare Text 64c1). e) The word self, which forms the first part of many hyphened compounds, is raised or lowered to conform to the position of the following part of the compound.

Short forms The principles by which familiar words of frequent occurrence may be abbreviated systematically to short forms which are almost obviously self-reading, are taught both in the Basic Text and the Dictation Course. 144 of the most useful applications of these principles are presented in the review chart on pages 40-41 of the Dictation Course, which combines the two review charts which appear on pages 124-125 and 134-135 of the Basic Text. 

In accord with the general statement that these same principles of abbreviation may be applied to occasional long words not specifically taught, a number of additional short forms have been introduced in Part 2 of the Dictation Course and in the Supplementary Dictation Exercises. Others, distinguished by the breve dot, have been taught as examples under that topic. In the course of editing this Dictionary, a few forms such as doctor dozen pound quarter yard, heretofore restricted to special limited use, have been "promoted" to general use; and about 15 new short forms have been suggested for general adoption. For convenient roference, as well as drill, all these additional short forms have been brought together in the review chart which appears on the next page. 

The 36 short forms which appear in the chart may be classified as follows. In these lists, the 15 forms not previously taught are starred.

 Successive vowels 

Script Shorthand often writes both of two successive vowels; more often omits the second; less often omits the first; in a few cases omits both. a) crayon chaos مي مدا AN b) c) d) lineament kaolin sahib idea studio vial

The usual conditions for writing both vowels are: a) For distinction from some other word. b) 

In rare words. c) 

In foreign words. d) Where the combination is final.

Both vowels may of course always be written, in words which are unfamiliar or of infrequent occurrence, merely for the sake of greater ease in reading. معاه سما e) serial various variety stadium easiest h... محمد strenuous TEL shower loyal

The usual conditions for omitting the second vowel are: e) Where the second vowel is short and unaccented. nh an to f) depreciate g) geology 216 ns че J Cl h) subsidiary casual patriotic judiciary gradual pronunciation The usual conditions for omitting the first vowel are: f) Where the first vowel is short and unaccented, and the second vowel is long.

C) Where the first vowel is short and unaccented, and the second vowel is accented.

The usual conditions for omitting both vowels are: h) In some words of more than 4 syllables, and in a few other cases, where neither vowel is accented.

Xav 

Unaccented medial vowels 

Three simple rules summarize

Script Shorthand policy regarding vowels; except, of course, for specific breves for words or affixes: 

1) Write initial and final vowels always. 

2) Write the principal accented vowel almost always.

Omit (or reduce to a) unaccented medial vowels with considerable freedom.

If the first two rules are strictly observed, the third offers a wide range for individual variation. Some writers will prefer fuller forms, giving maximum ease in reading; others will prefer shorter forms, giving minimum effort in writing. Either tendency is perfectly legitimate. The text-books and Dictionary lean to the side of fullness, giving maximum reading ease with ample writing speed for secretarial use; leaving it to the individual writer to further shorten forms of frequent occurrence in his own writing. 

Suggestions for reducing or omitting owels are summarized in Text 96 and in Dictation Course R16. These suggestions are amplified somewhat in this Introduction, chiefly under Vowel markings, Successive vowels, and Diphthongs.

These specific suggestions may be supplemented by two general principles: 1) Omit, or reduce, short vowels more freely than long vowels or diphthongs. 

2) Omit, or reduce, nedial vowels more freely in final or semi-final syllables than in the first, or second, syllable of a word.

Apply these principles with reasonable consistency, writing similar syllables similarly, so far as other considerations permit; and writing the same word always the same way, to eliminate mental hesitation.

In this particular respect---treatment of unaccented medial vowels--this Dictionary should not be regarded as showing the only correct or justifiable form, but rather as suggesting a normal standard for general use.

Introduction 

Changes A limited number of words appear in this Dictionary with forms differing in some respect from those which have appeared in the text-books and/or magazines. The principal changes are: 

About half of the 36 additional short forms summarized in this Introduction, together with a few of their derivatives A few of the words written with the 4 additional affixes presented in this Introduction. 

About 20 hyphened words for which single instead of separated forms are now given.

About a dozen words extending 3 units above the line, now written with single instead of separated forms. 

Chiefly, a substantial number of individual changes in the treatment of unaccented medial vowels in the interest of greater consistency and uniformity. In this connection note the statements in this Introduction under Unaccented medial vowels---especially the final paragraph. In general, these changes have tended in the direction of greater precision or approximation of spelling in proper names; toward more reduction or omission in all other words. 

Where a change involves a specific breve, or a word which has appeared in the text-books as a keyed example, the previous form for the root word is shown in its vocabulary place by a parenthetic entry immediately preceding the present form. 

XV

Arrangement and notation 

All words are arranged in a single strictly alphabetic order. 

Where a word has two markedly different accepted spellings (eg, fantom phantom, sirup: syrup), it is entered in both places.

Familiar breves commonly used in longhand are usually entered in parenthesis accompanying the full forn---e E, January(Jan.). 

The typed words show, in addition to the spelling, the word divisions which a careful typist would make at the end of a line. 

These word divisions conform to 5 simple rules: 1) 

Divide words only between syllables. 

2) Do not separate a one-letter syllable from the beginning or the end of a word. 

3) Do not separate a two-letter syllable from the end of a word. Note that according to these first 3 rules words of only one syllable (strength freight charge check), or of less than 5 letters (able also), may not be divided. 

Divide compound (hyphened) words only at the hyphen. 

Divide proper names less freely than other words. 4) 5) Do not, in general, separate a syllable of less than 3 letters from the beginning or the end of a. proper name. 

The ordinary single hyphen is used to indicate word divisions. 

The hyphen of compound words is written double, thus ༣ (able-bodied). (-----) (2) (A) used:

Words in parenthesis call attention to shorthand forms not now used which have appeared heretofore in text-books. (See last paragraph under Changes, page xv.)
